The crystal of semistandard rigid tableaux R ⁡ ( ϖ ~ 2 ) R(\widetilde{\varpi}_{2}) (left) and R ⁡ ( ϖ ~ 3 ) R(\widetilde{\varpi}_{3}) (right) in type B 3 B_{3} . Remark 6.9 . We can also give a bijective proof of Proposition 5.33 . Indeed, we recall from [ Gordon83 ] that ∏ 1 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ n r + i + j − 1 i + j − 1 \prod_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}\dfrac{r+i+j-1}{i+j-1} equals the number of semistandard Young tableaux with at most r r columns and entries in { 1 , … , n } \{1,\dotsc,n\} . Note that any SSRT in R ⁡ ( r ​ ϖ n ) R(r\varpi_{n}) is precisely the dual conjugate of such a semistandard tableau. Hence, Theorem 6.8 implies Proposition 5.33 . More explicitly, we reflect each entry t t about the y = − x y=-x line and replace it with n + 1 − t n+1-t . As an example, note the tableaux for R ⁡ ( ϖ ~ 3 ) R(\widetilde{\varpi}_{3}) in Figure 6 . 6.2. Motzkin triangle numbers In this section, we shall give various realization of Motzkin triangular numbers ℳ ( m , s ) \mathcal{M}_{(m,s)} in term of SSRTs. For 0 ≤ s ≤ n − 1 0\leq s\leq n-1 and any highest weight element T ♮ = ( τ ( 1 ) , τ ( 2 ) , τ ( 3 ) ) T^{\natural}=(\tau^{(1)},\tau^{(2)},\tau^{(3)}) of highest weight ϖ n + ϖ ~ n − s \varpi_{n}+\widetilde{\varpi}_{n-s} , let us denote by R ⁡ ( T ♮ ) R(T^{\natural}) the closure under e i e_{i} and f i f_{i} containing T ♮ T^{\natural} . Recall that a tableau T T is standard if T T has n n boxes and each i ∈ { 1 , … , n } i\in\{1,\dotsc,n\} appears exactly once in T T . Define the subset S ( T ♮ ) m : ⁣ = { T ∈ R ( T ♮ ) ∣ 𝖲𝗁 ⁡ ( T ) ⊢ m and T is standard } . S(T^{\natural})_{m}\mathbin{:=}\{T\in R(T^{\natural})\mid\text{$\mathsf{Sh}(T)\vdash m$ and $T$ is standard}\}. Then Theorem 5.12 can be restated as follows: | S ​ ( T ♮ ) m | = ℳ ( m , s ) . \lvert S(T^{\natural})_{m}\rvert=\mathcal{M}_{(m,s)}. In this section, for each ( m , s ) (m,s) , we shall show that there are distinct ( s + 1 ) (s+1) -many sets of standard rigid tableaux whose cardinalities are the same as ℳ ( m , s ) \mathcal{M}_{(m,s)} . In this section we fix ϖ = ϖ n + ϖ ~ n − s \varpi=\varpi_{n}+\widetilde{\varpi}_{n-s} . We shall start with the following lemma which is easy to check: Lemma 6.10 . In type B n B_{n} , we have B ​ ( ϖ n ) ⊗ 3 ≅ ⨁ s = 0 n B ​ ( ϖ n + ϖ ~ n − s ) ⊕ s + 1 . B(\varpi_{n})^{\otimes 3}\cong\bigoplus_{s=0}^{n}B(\varpi_{n}+\widetilde{\varpi}_{n-s})^{\oplus s+1}. Furthermore, for ϖ = ϖ n + ϖ ~ n − s \varpi=\varpi_{n}+\widetilde{\varpi}_{n-s} , the highest crystal elements are given by O ϖ t = ( ∅ , λ ⁡ ( t ) , λ ⁡ ( s , t + 1 ) ) for 0 ≤ t ≤ s . O^{t}_{\varpi}=(\emptyset,\uplambda(t),\uplambda(s;t+1))\quad\text{ for }\quad 0\leq t\leq s. Note that for ϖ = ϖ n + ϖ ~ n − s \varpi=\varpi_{n}+\widetilde{\varpi}_{n-s} , the inner shape of O ϖ t O^{t}_{\varpi} is ( s , s − t ) (s,s-t) . Next, we denote R t ( ϖ n + ϖ ~ n − s ) : ⁣ = R n , 3 ( ( s , s − t ) ) R^{t}(\varpi_{n}+\widetilde{\varpi}_{n-s})\mathbin{:=}R_{n,3}\bigl((s,s-t)\bigr) . Theorem 6.11 . For 0 ≤ s ≤ n 0\leq s\leq n , the subcrystal R t ​ ( ϖ n + ϖ ~ n − s ) R^{t}(\varpi_{n}+\widetilde{\varpi}_{n-s}) is isomorphic to B ⁡ ( ϖ n + ϖ ~ n − s ) B(\varpi_{n}+\widetilde{\varpi}_{n-s}) of type B n B_{n} . Proof. Since R t ​ ( ϖ n + ϖ ~ n − s ) R^{t}(\varpi_{n}+\widetilde{\varpi}_{n-s}) is stable under the Kashiwara operators by Proposition 6.5 , Lemma 6.10 and the pigeonhole principle implies that the same argument of the second proof of Theorem 6.6 holds. ∎ Note that R 0 ​ ( ϖ n + ϖ ~ n − s ) = R ⁡ ( O ϖ n + ϖ ~ n − s ) R^{0}(\varpi_{n}+\widetilde{\varpi}_{n-s})=R(O_{\varpi_{n}+\widetilde{\varpi}_{n-s}}) in the previous section. Then the following corollary follows from Theorem 5.12 . Corollary 6.12 . Remark 6.14 . Theorem 5.12 and Lemma 6.10 imply the identity (6.3) 3 m = ∑ s = 0 m ( s + 1 ) ​ ℳ ( m , s ) \displaystyle 3^{m}=\sum_{s=0}^{m}(s+1)\mathcal{M}_{(m,s)} without appealing to Schur–Weyl duality. Indeed, note that the number of strict partitions ( τ ( 1 ) , τ ( 2 ) , τ ( 3 ) ) (\tau^{(1)},\tau^{(2)},\tau^{(3)}) satisfying τ ( 1 ) ∪ τ ( 2 ) ∪ τ ( 3 ) = λ ⁡ ( m ) \tau^{(1)}\cup\tau^{(2)}\cup\tau^{(3)}=\uplambda(m) is 3 m 3^{m} . 6.3. Extension to type C n C_{n} Now we assume that 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} is of type C n C_{n} for this section. Recall that there exists a virtualization map on highest weight crystals from type C n C_{n} to B n B_{n} given by e i ↦ e ^ i γ i , f i ↦ f ^ i γ i , ϖ i ↦ γ i ​ ϖ ^ i , e_{i}\mapsto\widehat{e}_{i}^{\gamma_{i}},\qquad\qquad f_{i}\mapsto\widehat{f}_{i}^{\gamma_{i}},\qquad\qquad\varpi_{i}\mapsto\gamma_{i}\widehat{\varpi}_{i}, where γ i = 1 + δ i ​ n \gamma_{i}=1+\delta_{in} (see, e.g. , [ K96 , OSS03III , OSS03II , SchillingS15 ] ) and for each object X X in type C n C_{n} , we write the corresponding object in type B n B_{n} as X ^ \widehat{X} . Thus, we can realize a highest weight crystal B ⁡ ( λ ) B(\lambda) as a subset of semistandard rigid tableaux R ⁡ ( O λ ) R(O_{\lambda}) . Proposition 6.15 . In type C n C_{n} , we have dim V ⁡ ( r ​ ϖ n ) = ∏ 1 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ n 2 ​ r + i + j i + j . \dim V(r\varpi_{n})=\prod_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}\dfrac{2r+i+j}{i+j}. 7. Semistandard spin rigid tableaux In this section, we assume 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} is of type D n D_{n} and use the notation of semistandard spin rigid tableaux from [ KLO17 ] . We will describe the corresponding crystal structure on semistandard spin rigid tableaux and give some applications. 7.1. Definition A colored integer is an integer with a color, gray or white. Remark 7.1 . When we do not want to tell the color of a colored integer, we write it without circle; that is, 𝐧 \mathbf{n} can be n or n . Also we assign an integer c ⁡ ( 𝐧 ) = 1 \mathrm{c}(\mathbf{n})=1 if 𝐧 \mathbf{n} is gray and c ⁡ ( 𝐧 ) = 0 \mathrm{c}(\mathbf{n})=0 if 𝐧 \mathbf{n} is white. Definition 7.2 . For colored integers 𝐣 \mathbf{j} and 𝐤 \mathbf{k} , we construct two partial orders: (1) 𝐣 ⪰ 𝐤 \mathbf{j}\succeq\mathbf{k} if and only if their colors coincide ( c ⁡ ( 𝐣 ) = c ⁡ ( 𝐤 ) ) (\mathrm{c}(\mathbf{j})=\mathrm{c}(\mathbf{k})) and | 𝐣 | ≥ | 𝐤 | |\mathbf{j}|\geq|\mathbf{k}| ; (2) 𝐣 > 𝐤 \mathbf{j}>\mathbf{k} if and only if | 𝐣 | > | 𝐤 | |\mathbf{j}|>|\mathbf{k}| . Definition 7.3 . For a sequence of colored integers τ = ( τ 1 , τ 2 , … , τ s ) \uptau=(\uptau_{1},\uptau_{2},\ldots,\uptau_{s}) , we say τ \uptau a alternating strict partition (ASP) if (1) c ⁡ ( τ i ) ≠ c ⁡ ( τ i + 1 ) \mathrm{c}(\uptau_{i})\neq\mathrm{c}(\uptau_{i+1}) for all i i , (2) τ i > τ i + 1 \uptau_{i}>\uptau_{i+1} . Definition 7.4 . By the tensor product rule of crystals, we can define the crystal structure on the sequence of ASPs. From Equation ( 2.3a ) and ( 2.3c ), any highest weight crystal B ⁡ ( λ ) B(\lambda) appears as a subcrystal of B ​ ( ϖ n ) ⊗ a ⊗ B ​ ( ϖ n + 1 ) ⊗ b B(\varpi_{n})^{\otimes a}\otimes B(\varpi_{n+1})^{\otimes b} for some a , b ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} . Thus any B ⁡ ( λ ) B(\lambda) can be realized as certain set of sequence of ASPs. For a positive colored integer 𝐦 \mathbf{m} , we denote by λ ⁡ ( 𝐦 ) \uplambda(\mathbf{m}) the ASP given by λ ⁡ ( 𝐦 ) = ( 𝐦 , 𝐦 − 𝟏 , … , 𝟐 , 𝟏 ) . \uplambda(\mathbf{m})=(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}-\mathbf{1},\ldots,\mathbf{2},\mathbf{1}). More generally, for a ≥ b ≥ 1 a\geq b\geq 1 , we we denote by λ ⁡ ( 𝐚 , 𝐛 ) \uplambda(\mathbf{a};\mathbf{b}) the ASP given by λ ⁡ ( 𝐚 , 𝐛 ) = ( 𝐚 , 𝐚 − 𝟏 , … , 𝐛 ) \uplambda(\mathbf{a};\mathbf{b})=(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}) such that if j ≡ k mod 2 j\equiv k\mod{2} , then c ⁡ ( 𝐣 ) = c ⁡ ( 𝐤 ) \mathrm{c}(\mathbf{j})=\mathrm{c}(\mathbf{k}) . If b > a b>a , then we define λ ⁡ ( 𝐚 , 𝐛 ) = λ ⁡ ( 𝟎 ) \uplambda(\mathbf{a};\mathbf{b})=\uplambda(\mathbf{0}) with c ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) = c ⁡ ( 𝟎 ) \mathrm{c}(\mathbf{a})=\mathrm{c}(\mathbf{0}) . Note that if a ≡ b mod 2 a\equiv b\mod{2} , then c ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) = c ⁡ ( 𝐛 ) \mathrm{c}(\mathbf{a})=\mathrm{c}(\mathbf{b}) . Next, we define the notion of a semistandard spin rigid tableau of shape μ / η \mu/\eta (in short SSSRT) to be a skew-tableaux of shape μ / η \mu/\eta that is weakly decreasing along columns and strictly decreasing along rows. As we did for SSRTs, we can assign two partitions μ ⊃ η \mu\supset\eta for each SSSRT, in the following way: for each 1 ≤ i ≤ ℓ − 1 1\leq i\leq\ell-1 , there exists a unique t i ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 t_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} such that τ ( i ) ⊇ τ > t i ( i + 1 ) \uptau^{(i)}\supseteq\uptau^{(i+1)}_{>t_{i}} and τ ( i ) ⊉ τ > t i − 2 ( i + 1 ) \uptau^{(i)}\not\supseteq\uptau^{(i+1)}_{>t_{i}-2} for 1 ≤ i ≤ k − 1 1\leq i\leq k-1 , Then we set η i = ∑ s = i ℓ − 1 t s ( 1 ≤ i ≤ ℓ − 1 ) and μ j = η j + ℓ ⁡ ( τ ( j ) ) ( 1 ≤ j ≤ ℓ ) . \eta_{i}=\displaystyle\sum_{s=i}^{\ell-1}t_{s}\ \ (1\leq i\leq\ell-1)\quad\text{ and }\quad\mu_{j}=\eta_{j}+\ell(\uptau^{(j)})\ \ (1\leq j\leq\ell). Moreover, we can consider the entries of the i i -th row of the corresponding SSSRT as τ ( i ) \uptau^{(i)} . Thus, we identify sequences of ASPs and SSSRTs. Example 7.6 . We say that a composition λ \lambda of m m is almost even when it satisfies one of the following conditions: • If m m is odd, then it contains one odd part and the other parts are even. • If m m is even, then it contains two odd parts and the other parts are even. Our initial approach in trying to prove Proposition 5.30 was based on using the LGV lemma and a crystal model on non-intersecting lattice paths. Indeed, we consider the infinite Z 2 grid as the infinite board with tiles given by Figure . Figure 8 Figure 8 8 Figure 8 ⋱ ⋱ ⋱ ⋱ ⋱ ⋱ ⋱ ⋱ ⋱ ⋱ ⋱ ⋱ ⋱ ⋱ ⋱ ⋱ ⋱ ⋱ ⋱ ⋱ 1 1 ⋮ ⋮ - n 2 - n 1 n n - n 1 - n 2 1 1 ⋮ ⋮ - n 2 - n 1 n n - n 1 - n 2 1 1 ⋮ ⋮ - n 2 - n 1 n n - n 1 - n 2 1 1 ⋮ ⋮ - n 2 - n 1 n n - n 1 - n 2 1 1 ⋮ ⋮ - n 2 - n 1 n n - n 1 - n 2 ( 0 , 0 ) 8The board used to construct the non-intersecting lattice path model for crystals. The black dot denotes the origin ∈ ( 0 , 0 ) Z 2 . Figure 8The board used to construct the non-intersecting lattice path model for crystals. The black dot denotes the origin ∈ ( 0 , 0 ) Z 2 . Next, we define a crystal structure on the non-intersecting lattice paths on this board as follows. An addable (resp. removable) i -corner is a path that has a corner on the south-east (resp. north-west) corner of an i tile. We define the good addable (resp. removable) i -corner by using the signature rule given by reading top-to-bottom, left-to-right, where an addable i -corner is a + and a removable i -corner is a - and add a second ± if we can add (resp. remove) a second n -corner. The good addable (resp. removable) i -corner corresponds to the leftmost + (resp. rightmost - ) in the reduced signature. The crystal structure is given by having an f i removing the good removable corner and e i adding the good addable corner. Let L n , i denote the set of lattice paths from ( 0 , 0 ) to ( i , + - ⁢ 2 n i 1 ) with the aforementioned crystal structure. We leave it to the interested reader to construct an explicit crystal isomorphism : ϕ i → L n , i B ( ~ ϖ i ) . Furthermore, for a dominant integral weight = λ ∑ ∈ i I ⁢ c i ~ ϖ i , consider the sequence = ( i k ) = k 0 - ℓ 1 ( ⏟ n , … , n c n , … , ⏟ 2 , … , 2 c 2 , ⏟ 1 , … , 1 c 1 ) , where = ℓ ∑ ∈ i I c i . Let L ( λ ) denote the set of non-intersecting lattice paths from ( k , - k ) to ( + i k k , - + - ⁢ 2 n i k 1 k ) with the crystal structure above. It is straightforward to see that this is compatible with the tensor product rule in L n , i 0 ⊗ ⋯ ⊗ L n , i - ℓ 1 . Hence, we obtain ≅ L ( λ ) B ( λ ) by applying ϕ i to every factor L n , i . We note that introducing t to count the negative powers of q to obtain Theorem 5.3 is artificial. Indeed, it was not motivated representation theory. Problem 8.10 8.10 8.10 Problem 8.10 Problem 8.10. Give a natural representation theoretic interpretation of the q , t -Catalan triangle numbers ⁢ C ( n , k ) ( q , t ) . One possible interpretation of the q , t version of our paths is coming from the branching rule coming from the natural embedding → ⁢ s p ⁢ 2 n ⁢ s l ⁢ 2 n . Indeed, recall that we can relate characters ⁢ χ ( λ ) ( x 1 , … , x n ) in type C n with a specialized type A - ⁢ 2 n 1 character ⁢ χ ( λ ) ( x 1 , x 1 - 1 , x 2 , x 2 - 1 , … , x n , x n - 1 ) (see, e.g., [KT87, Lemma 1.5.1]). Our proof of Theorem 5.8 is essentially algebraic: showing that our combinatorial objects with their statistics satisfy the recursion relation for q -binomial coefficients. However, q -binomials [ + n m n ] q have an interpretation as generating function for the size of a partition in an × n m rectangle. Thus, we have the following problem. Proof. This follows from the LGV lemma and taking the weight of the vertical edge e to be q a , where a is the number of vertical steps directly to the left of e . The LGV approach does not naturally extend to show Conjecture as the only statistic that we can see that results in Cigler’s q -Motzkin number is tunnel length. Indeed, recall that a tunnel for a Motzkin path is the positions ( i , j ) of a pair of matching parentheses 6 footnote 6 6 footnote 6 Recall that we can represent a Motzkin path as a sequence of parentheses with a letter x such that every open parenthesis ‘(’ has a matching closing parenthesis ‘)’. and the length of the tunnel is + - j i 1 . Thus, define the tunnel length of M as = T ( M ) + - ∑ ( i , j ) j i 1 , where the sum is over all tunnels ( i , j ) of M . The following proposition is straightforward from the recursion on Motzkin paths for the first return to = y 0 . Proposition A.5 A.5 A.5 Proposition A.5 Proposition A.5. We have = ⁢ M † n ( q ) ∑ M q T ( M ) , where the sum is over all Motzkin paths of length M . Note that this is not compatible with the LGV lemma as we cannot assign a weighting to each edge that agrees with the tunnel length. Contrast this with area for Dyck paths which yields Proposition . Hence, to prove Conjecture combinatorially, one would need a statistic on Motzkin paths that can be considered as an edge weighting on the corresponding digraph to generated Motzkin paths. Alternative proof using the LGV for Catalan numbers. The result follows from LGV lemma and a bijection with non-intersecting lattice paths and type C n King tableaux given by using Ξ + n 1 on each path P ( i ) , with an appropriate shift of starting point, yielding the i -th column. It is straightforward to see that the non-intersecting condition corresponds to semistandard condition on King tableaux, similar to the case for the Jacobi–Trudi formula. ∎ Alternative proof using elementary manipulations.
